power_id text
1 We conducted a series of power analyses to determine an appropriate number of both countries (K = 20, 25, 30, 32) and participants per country (N = 150, 200, 250, 300). To assess the expected power, we set α = .05. We used the simr package in R to run 1,000 simulations of the planned mixed-models specified in our analysis plan 47 . Model parameters were estimated based on previous literature and our pilot study. The simulations utilized the Kenward-Roger approximation for fixed effects testing to ensure robustness. See the Supplementary Information for power curves for both hypotheses across the full parameter space.
2;3 Table 1. Design table Hypotheses Outcome Measures Sampling Plan (N, power analyses) Analysis Plan Interpretation H1 (country-level): individuals from countries higher in emotional expressivity will show stronger emotion overgeneralization, such that the association between happinessresemblance scores and trustworthiness ratings will be stronger. DV: Individual Fisher z-transformed emotion overgeneralization (participant-level Pearson r between happinessresemblance scores of 120 face stimuli and trustworthiness ratings, Fisher ztransformed). Predictor: countrylevel mean emotional expressivity (Abridged Display Rule Assessment Inventory). 32 countries (K = 32), 200 participants/country (N ≈ 6,400). Power analysis via simr (1,000 simulations, α = .05) shows 95.2% power to detect b = 0.16 for the Level-2 predictor. Power for Level-2 effects is primarily driven by K rather than N; K = 32 is substantial by cross-cultural standards. Stratified by gender, age (18-65), region within country; recruited via online panel providers. Multilevel linear model, individuals (L1) nested in countries (L2). DV: Fisher z overgeneralization. Predictor: country-mean expressivity (L2). Covariates: age, gender (L1); ethnic fractionalization (L2). Random intercepts for country. Robustness checks: recompute overgeneralization within each face-race subset (White/Black/Latino/As ian); re-run using CFD norming data as alternative happinessresemblance proxy. Positive: supports the prediction that countrylevel expressivity is associated with stronger overgeneralization, consistent with ecological calibration. Null: no evidence for a cross-cultural expressivityovergeneralization link; interpreted as lack of support for countrylevel calibration. Negative: contradicts the proposed direction, suggesting different ecological dynamics than hypothesized. H2 (individuallevel): Within countries, individuals with greater emotional expressivity will show stronger emotion overgeneralization. Same DV as H1. Predictors: groupmean centered individual expressivity (withincountry effect) and country-mean expressivity (between-country control). Same dataset as H1. Power via simr (1,000 simulations, α = .05) exceeds 95% to detect a small effect (b = 0.10) at K = 32, N = 200, under a conservative random-slope-variance assumption (0.0144, allowing ~20% of countries to show a reversed association). Multilevel model. DV: Fisher z overgeneralization. Predictor: group-mean centered individual expressivity (withincountry); country-mean expressivity (betweencountry covariate). Covariates: age, gender (L1); ethnic fractionalization (L2). Random effects for country (maximal structure attempted; simplified to random intercepts only if convergence fails). Robustness checks: same as H1. Positive within-country effect: supports the individual-level internalization account-individual differences in perceived expressivity predict overgeneralization even net of country. Null: no evidence that perceived norms relate to overgeneralization within countries; consistent with a more invariant account at the individual level. Negative: contradicts the proposed direction, suggesting alternative mechanisms or measurement issues.
4 We conducted simulation-based power analyses using the simr package in R 47, following the planned multilevel models described in the main text. Analyses were implemented using R scripts adapted from prior work 53, with modifications to match the present design. Full simulation code and output tables are provided to facilitate reproducibility (see the Code Availability section).
5 We conducted a sensitivity analysis by varying the effect size (b = 0.05, 0.10, 0.15) and the random slope variance for expressivity (0.0064, 0.0144, 0.0256). We specifically tested a slope variance of 0.0144 (SD = 0.12) as a conservative estimate; this value accounts for potential cultural heterogeneity where approximately 20% of countries might theoretically exhibit an association in the opposite direction. At K = 32 and N = 200, we achieved power > 95% for a small effect of b = 0.10 even under a conservative variance assumption of 0.0144 (Figure S4).

ISO = International Organization for Standardization country codes. Cultural group classifications follow the Inglehart-Welzel cultural map 45, and are included to illustrate the breadth of cultural coverage, not as analytic categories. These broad typologies do not imply within-region homogeneity. For example, Kenya and Nigeria, classified here within the African-Islamic cluster, are religiously diverse and include substantial Christian populations. Expressivity levels are sourced from Matsumoto et al. 32 and were operationalized as the mean frequency with which participants selected “express it as you feel it” as the most appropriate behavior across social scenarios. ‘n/a’ indicates countries not included in the original Matsumoto dataset. sampled countries. The figure displays the distribution of the 32 sampled countries (dark grey) relative to the global reference distribution (light grey) across three key dimensions: cultural distance from the United States 44 (left panel), GLOBE In-group Collectivism 42 (societal practices; middle panel), and Hofstede’s individualism index 43 (right panel). As shown, the selected sample spans a wide range of values on all three dimensions, indicating substantial cross-country heterogeneity in cultural contexts. detecting the effect of country-level expressivity on overgeneralization as a function of effect size (b) and number of countries (K). Panels correspond to the number of participants per country (N). Points represent mean power across 1,000 simulations, with vertical lines indicating 95% confidence intervals. Horizontal dashed and dotted lines denote 95% and 90% power thresholds, respectively. Under the planned design (K = 32, N = 200), power reached 95.2% for an expected effect size of b = 0.16. Curves for K = 30 are omitted for visual clarity; this condition was included in the simulations but is not shown in the figure.
7;8 All simulations used 1,000 iterations, with statistical significance evaluated at α = .05 and fixed effects tested using the Kenward-Roger approximation. Power was estimated across a range of design parameters, including the number of countries (K = 20, 25, 30, 32), the number of participants per country (N = 150, 200, 250, 300), and plausible effect sizes informed by pilot data and prior literature. For H1, the primary simulation used an expected effect of b = 0.16, based on the pilot study. For H2, we additionally varied the random slope variance for individual-level expressivity (0.0064, 0.0144, 0.0256), corresponding to slope standard deviations of 0.08, 0.12, and 0.16.
9 Power curves for H2 across parameter space. Estimated statistical power for detecting the effect of individual-level expressivity on overgeneralization as a function of random slope variance and number of countries (K). Panels are arranged by effect size (b; rows) and number of participants per country (N; columns). Points represent mean power across 1,000 simulations, with vertical lines indicating 95% confidence intervals. Horizontal dashed and dotted lines denote 95% and 90% power thresholds, respectively. Under the planned design (K = 32, N = 200), power exceeded 95% for detecting a small effect (b = 0.10) under a conservative slope variance assumption (0.0144). Curves for K = 30 are omitted for visual clarity; this condition was included in the simulations but is not shown in the figure.
10 In sum, these simulations suggest that a sample of 200 participants per country across 32 countries (N = 6,400) provides sufficient power to detect our primary effects of interest. This target ensures we are well-powered for individual-level effects while maintaining a rigorous and feasible sample for country-level comparisons.
11;12 For H1, we examined the effect of country-level expressivity on face-based overgeneralization. As this hypothesis involves a level-2 predictor, power is primarily driven by the number of countries (K) rather than the number of participants (N). With K = 30 and N = 200, the estimated power was 93.6%, slightly below the prespecified 95% threshold. We therefore increased the number of countries to K = 32, which yielded 95.2% power for this effect (Figure S3). K = 32 is also comparable to, or larger than, those commonly used in cross-cultural psychological research 48, 49.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 1 non-significant p value that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Text Sentence
p = .867 We compared a random-intercept model with a model including Model comparison indicated no evidence for random slope variation, Δχ²(2) = 0.29, p = .867.

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Code Comments

Best programming practice is to add comments to code, to explain what the code does (to yourself in the future, or peers who want to re-use your code). All your code files had comments.

Missing Files

All files loaded in the code were present in the repository.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In all code files, libraries/imports were loaded in one block.

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 9 distinct packages/libraries: bruceR, furrr, ggrepel, lmerTest, patchwork, psych, simr, sjPlot, tidyverse. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing an renv.lock (via the renv package), a sessionInfo() text dump, or a groundhog/checkpoint date-pin alongside the code.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 50 columns across 6 data files.

Issues Identified

1 file/column combination has at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

File Column Issues
facerating_raw.csv user_id 🔒 Personally Identifying Information — Column name suggests personal information (matched: userid)

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 6 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A script runs longer than our limit of 5 minutes.

  • power_simulation.Rmd

State in the README how long each script runs. If some steps take hours, save their results (such as fitted models) to files, add those to the deposit, and let the script read them, so readers can check the rest without waiting.

A script waits for a person to choose a file, answer a question, or give arguments on the command line.

  • pilot_analysis.R
  • pilot_analysis.Rmd

Nobody can answer when the code is checked automatically. Write the file name into the script, and put the exact command with its arguments in the README.

>     subject <- 1:(N*K)
>     
>     # outcome (placeholder); actual responses will be generated internally by simr
>     overgeneralization <- rnorm(N*K, mean = 0, sd = 1) 
>     
>     # predictor: country-level expressivity
>     country_expressivity <- qnorm(ppoints(K)) 
>     expressivity <- rep(country_expressivity, each = N)
> 
>     ## bind variables into dataframe
>     df_rq1 <-
>         data.frame(
>             country = factor(country),
>             subject = factor(subject),
>             outcome = overgeneralization,
>             predictor = expressivity 
>         )
>     
>     model_rq1 <-
>         simr::makeLmer(
>                   outcome ~ 1 + predictor + 
>                       (1 | country), 
>                   fixef = c(0, beta),
>                   VarCorr = 0.05, # var of country random intercept, calculated based on pilot icc
>                   sigma = 1,
>                   data = df_rq1
>               )
> 
>     ## check power
>     sim_rq1 <- 
>         powerSim(
>             model_rq1,
>             test = simr::fixed("predictor", "kr"), 
>             nsim = n_sim,
>             alpha = 0.05
>         )
> 
>     ## create table with powerSim output and info on variables
>     summary(sim_rq1) %>%
>     mutate(subjects = N,
>            countries = K,
>            beta = beta,
>            class = "rq1"
>            )
> }
> parameter_space_rq1 <-
>     crossing(
>         N = c(150, 200, 250, 300), # number of subjects per country
>         K = c(20, 25, 32), # number of countries
>         beta = c(0.10, 0.16, 0.20) # beta for "predictor" 
>     )
> pwr_rq1 <-
>     future_pmap_dfr(parameter_space_rq1,
>                     simulation_swarm_rq1,
>                     .options = furrr_options(seed = TRUE),
>                     .progress = TRUE
>                     )
