How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Judgments of trustworthiness from faces guide critical social decisions. It remains debated whether these judgments reflect shared features of human perception or are shaped by cultural experience. One prominent explanation is emotion overgeneralization: faces that structurally resemble emotional expressions elicit corresponding impressions, even when no emotion is displayed. Although often treated as universal, emotion overgeneralization may reflect ecological learning shaped by culture-specific norms. We propose a cultural gradient: where emotional expressions are more informative, the link between happiness resemblance and perceived trustworthiness should be stronger. Participants from 32 countries (N ≈ 6,400) will evaluate the trustworthiness of unfamiliar individuals from standardized resting facial photographs and complete a measure of emotional expressivity, with each face's resemblance to happiness quantified computationally. We will test whether emotional expressivity is associated with greater emotion overgeneralization at both country and individual levels. Null results would favor perceptual invariance, whereas positive associations would support ecological calibration.

Introduction

Judgments of trustworthiness are fundamental to human life, serving as a cornerstone for social cooperation and collective action 1, 2 . Facial appearances shape these judgments, with perceivers forming stable impressions after only brief exposure 3 . These facial impressions can override more valid behavior information 4 , and predict various high-stakes outcomes, ranging from consumer decisions to criminal sentencing [5] [6] [7] . Although trustworthiness judgments from faces have been studied extensively, they are typically treated as reflecting universal perceptual processes, with limited consideration of whether their magnitude is shaped by social ecology.

This issue reflects a long-standing question in social perception: to what extent are person perception processes shared across humans, and to what extent are they shaped by the cultural and ecological environments in which people develop? Evidence for both perspectives has accumulated. On one hand, analyses of a large-scale dataset found little systematic variation in facial trait impressions across world regions, suggesting that individual differences, rather than cultural background, account for the meaningful variation in impressions 8 . Similarly, the basic structure of social judgments appears to be relatively consistent across regions 9 . On the other hand, other work has identified variation in social perception associated with cultural and environmental factors. For example, the structure of face impressions varies with regional personality profiles 10 , while individual differences in face-based trust evaluations are shaped more strongly by environmental than genetic factors 11.

Thus, cross-cultural similarity in social impressions does not necessarily imply that the processes underlying these impressions operate with equal strength. Addressing these mixed findings requires theory-driven predictions about when and why social-perceptual processes should vary across cultures.

One prominent mechanism underlying face-based trustworthiness impressions is emotion overgeneralization, a bias grounded in the classic overgeneralization account whereby perceivers interpret momentary characteristics of a person as enduring traits 12 . Driven by an oversensitive emotion detection system, this bias extends beyond overt expressions to subtle structural facial resemblances [13] [14] [15] . For example, an individual with an emotionally "neutral" (resting) face resembling happy expressions is judged as more trustworthy 16, 17 , and computational approaches identified emotional resemblance as the strongest predictor of trustworthiness judgments 18.

Emotion overgeneralization has been observed across diverse populations, showing that people across cultures spontaneously derive trait impressions from emotional cues 19 . Influential accounts have treated emotion overgeneralization as a species-typical perceptual tendency shared across humans, emphasizing its universality [20] [21] [22].

However, cross-cultural presence does not imply invariance in magnitude. Existing evidence is drawn predominantly from U.S. samples, where emotional expressions tend to be frequent and socially informative. We extend this perspective: we propose that, although the underlying perceptual tendency may be universal, its strength is shaped by ecological experience.

Emotion overgeneralization may reflect ecological learning: social-perceptual systems are calibrated to cues that reliably predict important social outcomes within a given environment 23, 24.

Emotional expressions provide such cues because they often signal others' intentions. Positive affective states are associated with prosocial tendencies 25, 26 , and smiles, for example, can signal approachability and cooperative intent, whereas negative expressions can signal potential threat 21, 22, 24 . Through repeated experience, individuals learn associations between emotional expressions and underlying intentions, and these learned associations can generalize to resting faces that share similar structural features 13 . The strength of these associations, however, should depend on the frequency and reliability of expression-intention pairings in a given ecology. Supporting this, real-world face-based trait inference is malleable to the statistical learning of cooccurrence of facial appearances and behavioral outcomes 27.

Emotional expressivity represents one important ecological factor shaping these learning environments. Defined as the extent to which emotional states are normatively encouraged to be openly displayed 28 , expressivity influences both the frequency and interpretability of emotional signals. In cultures where emotional expressions are normatively encouraged and reliably reflect authentic internal states, individuals encounter more frequent and consistent pairings between expressions and underlying intentions, strengthening the learned association between emotional cues and social judgments. In contrast, when emotional displays are more regulated or contextdependent, expressions may provide less reliable information about internal states, weakening their diagnostic value. Supporting this possibility, cultures differ in the social meanings of smiles.

Smiles can reflect pleasure, affiliation, or dominance, and the relative prominence of these functions varies across cultures 29, 30 . Smiling at strangers leads to good impressions in some countries, but impressions of lower competence in others 31 . Thus, emotional expressivity may calibrate the degree to which happiness-like facial features inform trustworthiness judgments, i.e., the magnitude of emotion overgeneralization.

At the individual level, even when broader social ecology is held constant, individuals differ in their personal histories of encountering expression-intention pairings, shaped by, for example, family expressivity norms, occupational context, and the composition of one's social network. There is substantial interindividual variation within countries in perceptions of emotional expressivity norms 32 . Those who perceive their environment as more expressively open have therefore been more consistently exposed to authentic smiling as a signal of benevolent intentions, strengthening their mappings between happiness-related cues and trustworthiness. Importantly, however, the consequences of individual differences in expressivity perceptions may depend on the broader cultural ecology in which these experiences occur: individual exposure to expressive cues should be most predictive of emotion overgeneralization when such cues are reliable and informative.

The present registered report provides a cross-cultural test of whether emotion overgeneralization in face-based trustworthiness judgments varies as a function of emotional expressivity at both the country and individual levels. We will collect data from approximately 6,400 participants across 32 countries spanning all inhabited continents (Figure 1). Participants will evaluate the trustworthiness of unfamiliar individuals from standardized resting facial photographs and complete a measure of emotional expressivity using the Abridged Display Rule Assessment Inventory 32, 33 . Computational modeling will quantify each face's resemblance to happiness-related facial configurations using a validated deep learning-based tool 34.

As shown in Table 1, at the country level, we test whether cultural differences in emotional expressivity are associated with variation in emotion overgeneralization. We hypothesize that individuals from countries higher in emotional expressivity will show stronger emotion overgeneralization, such that the association between happiness-resemblance scores and trustworthiness ratings will be stronger (H1). At the individual level, we test whether individual emotional expressivity is associated with emotion overgeneralization within countries. We hypothesize that within countries, individuals who have greater emotional expressivity will show stronger emotion overgeneralization (H2). Finally, as an exploratory analysis, we examine whether cultural context moderates the relationship between individual-level emotional expressivity and emotion overgeneralization. We predict that the positive association between individual emotional expressivity and emotion overgeneralization will be stronger in countries higher in emotional expressivity. selected for data collection are highlighted, with color indicating country-level emotional expressivity derived from Matsumoto's cross-cultural data 32 , where available. Countries without available expressivity data are shown in grey. Archival values from the 2008 dataset are shown to illustrate the expressivity range spanned by our country selection; for actual analysis, we will use DRAI scores collected in the present study, allowing us to examine emotional expressivity at both the individual level and the country level across all 32 countries.

Methods

Ethics information

The present research complies with all relevant ethical regulations. It has been approved by the Institutional Review Board of the National University of Singapore (NUS-IRB-2024-241 and DERC-000092). Informed consent will be obtained from all human participants. Participants will be paid in accordance with the standard of the online panel providers (e.g., Toluna).

Design

In our main study, participants from multiple countries will complete the tasks in the following order: (i) a face-rating task assessing perceived trustworthiness of unfamiliar individuals, (ii) questionnaires assessing emotional expressivity, and (iii) demographic information. The survey will be translated into the local languages following standard crosscultural translation procedures, including forward translation and back-translation to ensure semantic and conceptual equivalence across language versions. In addition to measures collected directly from participants, we will include derived measures, such as emotional resemblance scores for each face image (computed using automated facial analysis tools) and ethnic fractionalization (obtained from published, validated indices). As the study involves no experimental manipulation, all participants complete an identical task sequence, with no betweenor within-subjects conditions.

Trustworthiness ratings. Participants will rate 120 face images on trustworthiness. The face stimuli are drawn from prior cross-cultural work 35 and include emotionally neutral, frontfacing adult faces from the Chicago Face Database 36 . The final set contains equal numbers of male and female faces, and one-fourth each of White, Black, Latino, and Asian faces. The individuals photographed were instructed to face the camera directly with a neutral expression.

Background, lighting, and clothing were held constant across all images. Participants will rate the trustworthiness of each face using a scale ranging from 1 (not at all) to 9 (very) at their own pace.

Stimuli will appear in a randomized order.

Emotional resemblance. Emotional resemblance to happiness will be computed for each face stimulus using Py-Feat with ResMaskNet (Residual Masking Network), which applies a deep residual network combined with U-Net architecture to predict emotion probabilities 34, 37.

Emotion detectors were trained on an aggregate of diverse datasets encompassing Euro-American, African American, and Asian populations, providing greater demographic representativeness than single-site laboratory collections. The training protocols further enhance ecological validity by incorporating both laboratory-controlled posed expressions and naturalistic facial behaviors sourced from the internet 37 . These happiness probabilities will be combined with participants' trustworthiness ratings to calculate emotion overgeneralization scores (see Analysis Plan).

Emotional expressivity. Emotional expressivity will be measured using the Abridged Display Rule Assessment Inventory (DRAI), a psychometrically validated instrument designed to assess normative regulation of emotional expression across multiple interpersonal contexts 32, 33.

Expressive norms vary systematically across cultures, with display-rule norms capturing culturally shared expectations regarding emotional expression 32 . The instrument asks respondents about social norms governing emotional expression when they are alone and when interacting with five different social partners in both public and private settings. Respondents indicate which of six theoretically derived behaviors is most appropriate when experiencing anger, contempt, disgust, fear, happiness, sadness, or surprise. These response options correspond to the six expression-management modes identified by Ekman and Friesen 38, 39 : "show more than you feel it" (amplification), "express it as you feel it" (expression), "show the emotion while smiling at the same time" (qualification), "show less than you feel it" (deamplification), "hide your feelings by smiling" (masking), and "show nothing" (neutralization). In the present study, we will use the "express it as you feel it" index, defined as the total number of situations in which participants endorse authentic emotional expression. Here, we focus not simply on the overall frequency of emotional displays, but on the frequency of expressions that reliably co-occur with genuine internal states. Such expressions are more informative for learning expression-intention associations, whereas purely strategic or exaggerated displays introduce noise. Learning depends not only on the diagnosticity of a signal (i.e., accuracy) but also on the frequency of exposure to reliable pairings. Even when a signal is highly diagnostic (e.g., a rare smile is likely to be authentic), limited exposure to such pairings may constrain the formation of strong overgeneralized heuristics. We selected this index rather than the broader overall expressivity measure because it more directly captures normative endorsement of genuine emotional display, rather than tendencies toward amplification or suppression. Individual total scores will serve as the individual-level indicator, and country-level indicators will be derived by aggregating individual scores within each country.

Demographic information.

We will ask participants to report their gender, age, country of birth, length of stay in the country of data collection, and ethnic background. All demographic materials will be adjusted to the respective sites by local collaborators, ensuring that our questions will assess locally meaningful categories (e.g., ethnic background will be excluded in sites where the local political climate may prevent participants from sharing such information).

Ethnic fractionalization.

To account for potential confounding effects of cross-racial familiarity on participants' trait ratings, which may vary across countries despite a standardized stimulus set, we will include a measure of ethnic fractionalization 40 . This measure quantifies each country's ethnic heterogeneity as one minus the Herfindahl index of group shares, reflecting the probability of two randomly selected individuals from a population belonging to different ethnic groups. Higher fractionalization indicates greater ethnic diversity within a country. We include ethnic fractionalization as a country-level covariate to absorb cross-country differences in everyday face-exposure ecology, which could influence baseline trait calibration through mechanisms such as familiar face overgeneralization 15 ; we do not have a directional prediction for its association with overgeneralization.

Sampling plan

Sampling strategy. We plan to recruit participants from 32 countries worldwide (see Figure 1) through Toluna, with other established online panel providers used as backups. Within each country, the sampling will be stratified by gender, age (18-65 years), and region (where feasible). Countries were selected to capture broad cross-national variation in emotional expressivity while ensuring geographic coverage 41 . In addition, the selection includes countries that vary substantially on established cultural dimensions (e.g., individualism-collectivism 42, 43 and cultural distance from the United States 44 ) and cultural clusters (based on World Values Survey groupings 45, 46 ). This diversity in cultural and psychological dimensions is illustrated in Table S3 and Figure S2. Further details on country selection are provided in the Supplementary Information.

Power analysis.

We conducted a series of power analyses to determine an appropriate number of both countries (K = 20, 25, 30, 32) and participants per country (N = 150, 200, 250, 300). To assess the expected power, we set α = .05. We used the simr package in R to run 1,000 simulations of the planned mixed-models specified in our analysis plan 47 . Model parameters were estimated based on previous literature and our pilot study. The simulations utilized the Kenward-Roger approximation for fixed effects testing to ensure robustness. See the Supplementary Information for power curves for both hypotheses across the full parameter space.

For H1, we examined the effect of country-level expressivity on face-based overgeneralization. As this hypothesis involves a level-2 predictor, power is primarily driven by the number of countries (K) rather than the number of participants (N). With K = 30 and N = 200, the estimated power was 93.6%, slightly below the prespecified 95% threshold. We therefore increased the number of countries to K = 32, which yielded 95.2% power for this effect (Figure S3). K = 32 is also comparable to, or larger than, those commonly used in cross-cultural psychological research 48, 49.

For H2, we considered the effect of individual-level expressivity on overgeneralization.

To isolate the within-country effect, this predictor was group-mean centered within each country.

We conducted a sensitivity analysis by varying the effect size (b = 0.05, 0.10, 0.15) and the random slope variance for expressivity (0.0064, 0.0144, 0.0256). We specifically tested a slope variance of 0.0144 (SD = 0.12) as a conservative estimate; this value accounts for potential cultural heterogeneity where approximately 20% of countries might theoretically exhibit an association in the opposite direction. At K = 32 and N = 200, we achieved power > 95% for a small effect of b = 0.10 even under a conservative variance assumption of 0.0144 (Figure S4).

In sum, these simulations suggest that a sample of 200 participants per country across 32 countries (N = 6,400) provides sufficient power to detect our primary effects of interest. This target ensures we are well-powered for individual-level effects while maintaining a rigorous and feasible sample for country-level comparisons.

Inclusion and exclusion criteria. Participants must have normal or corrected-to-normal vision and must have been born in, and currently residing in, the country where data are collected.

To ensure data quality, exclusion will be applied if a participant meets any of the following criteria: (a) provides identical ratings on more than 90% of face-rating trials; (b) uses only two response options across all trait-rating trials, indicating a binary response pattern; or (c) fails all embedded attention checks. We target 200 analyzable participants per country (N = 6,400) and will oversample by 10% to account for expected participant exclusions.

Analysis plan

All analyses will be performed in R 50 . We operationalize emotion overgeneralization as a within-person association between stimulus-level happiness resemblance and trustworthiness ratings, which is invariant to individual differences in overall rating level. Specifically, for each participant, emotion overgeneralization will be indexed as the Pearson correlation coefficient between their trustworthiness ratings and the happiness resemblance scores of the 120 face stimuli. These individual-level correlation coefficients will be Fisher z-transformed prior to all inferential analyses and will serve as the primary dependent variable in all models. This index is the participant-specific slope relating happiness resemblance to trustworthiness ratings. Modeling this slope as a function of expressivity therefore tests whether the association between happiness resemblance and trustworthiness ratings varies as a function of expressivity, providing an intuitive participant-level summary while remaining closely aligned with the corresponding cross-level interaction in a trial-level model.

Outcome-neutral data quality checks. Before testing the preregistered hypotheses, we will conduct two outcome-neutral checks to assess whether the data provide a suitable basis for the planned analyses. First, we will test whether, when pooling across countries, happiness resemblance is positively associated with perceived trustworthiness. This analysis serves as a positive control to establish that the expected association between facial resemblance to happiness and trustworthiness is detectable in the overall sample. Second, we will report between-country variance in emotional expressivity scores to characterize the extent of crosscountry variation. These checks are independent of the hypothesized associations between emotional expressivity and emotion overgeneralization.

H1. Country-level analyses.

To test country-level hypothesis H1, whether country-level emotional expressivity is associated with cross-national variation in emotion overgeneralization, we will fit a multilevel linear model with individuals (Level 1) nested within countries (Level 2).

The dependent variable will be individual Fisher z-transformed emotion overgeneralization scores. The key predictor will be country-level mean emotional expressivity (Level 2). Countries will be included as random intercepts. Random slopes will not be specified because the predictor is defined at the country level and does not vary within countries. Participant age and gender (Level 1), and ethnic fractionalization (Level 2) will be included as covariates.

H2. Individual-level analyses. We will test individual-level hypothesis H2, whether individuals who perceive greater normative emotional expressivity will show stronger emotion overgeneralization. The dependent variable will be Fisher z-transformed emotion overgeneralization. The model will include group-mean centered individual expressivity (main predictor; within-country effect) and the corresponding country mean (to account for the between-country effect) to separate within-and between-country associations. Covariates will include participant age and gender (Level 1), and ethnic fractionalization (Level 2). Countries will be included as random effects. If the maximal random-effects structure fails to converge or produces singular fits, we will simplify the model following standard guidance for handling convergence failure in mixed models 51, 52 , retaining a model with random intercepts for countries if further simplification is required.

Exploratory analysis: Cross-level moderation.

As an exploratory analysis, we will examine whether country-level emotional expressivity moderates the association between individual emotional expressivity and emotion overgeneralization by fitting a multilevel linear model that includes the interaction between individual-level emotional expressivity and countrylevel mean emotional expressivity. Individual expressivity will be group-mean centered to capture within-country individual differences, whereas country-level emotional expressivity will be grand-mean centered. The dependent variable will be Fisher z-transformed emotion overgeneralization. Participant age and gender (Level 1), and ethnic fractionalization (Level 2) will be included as covariates. Countries will be included as random effects.

Robustness checks. We will compute overgeneralization separately within each face-race subset (White/Black/Latino/Asian; 30 faces each). Because each subset contains only 30 faces, participant-level estimates of overgeneralization may be less stable. We will therefore aggregate trustworthiness ratings at the country level before computing overgeneralization separately for each subset, and examine whether the country-level association replicates in direction across subsets. We will also use norming data from the Chicago Face Database as an alternative proxy for perceived happiness resemblance and re-run the analyses to evaluate the robustness of the findings.

Timeline

If the Stage 1 submission is accepted in principle (AIP), we anticipate the following timeline: • Formal institutional ethics approval, if required due to protocol changes during peer review: 1 month

Pilot data

To demonstrate the feasibility of our study design and analytic strategy for H1, we integrated two existing cross-cultural datasets: emotional expressivity scores from a prior study using the Display Rule Assessment Inventory 32 , and large-scale, face-based trustworthiness judgments from the Psychological Science Accelerator (PSA) project 35 . In the pilot data, emotional expressivity was operationalized as the total number of situations in which participants selected "express it as you feel it" as the most appropriate behavior across social scenarios, excluding the "amplify" responses that are included in the overall expressivity index. Twenty-one countries overlapped across datasets. Among the more than 10,000 participants in the PSA face perception dataset, each participant rated a single trait that was randomly assigned. Of these, 531 rated trustworthiness. For each face stimulus, structural resemblance to happiness was estimated using Py-Feat. Emotion overgeneralization was operationalized as the Fisher z-transformed correlation between happiness resemblance scores and trustworthiness ratings.

Because the expressivity predictor in this existing dataset is only available as a national aggregate, we first examined overgeneralization at the country level by aggregating trustworthiness ratings within each country. Greater endorsement of expressing emotions "as felt" predicted stronger emotion overgeneralization, b = 0.47, 95% CI [0.03, 0.90], SE = 0.21, t(18) = 2.27, p = .036, controlling for ethnic fractionalization, which serves as a proxy for cross-racial familiarity that may influence participants' trait ratings (Figure 2). However, the number of participants contributing trustworthiness ratings varied substantially across countries (Figure S1).

To account for this imbalance and retain the full participant-level information, we additionally analyzed the raw ratings using a mixed-effects model with participants nested within countries. Controlling for regional ethnic fractionalization, age, and gender, the association remained positive, b = 0.16, 95% CI [0.01, 0.32], SE = 0.07, t(10.4) = 2.29, p = .044. Full analytic details are reported in the Supplementary Information.

Importantly, this effect emerged only for the "express it as you feel it" index. Emotion overgeneralization was not significantly associated with the broader overall expressivity measure, which combines responses across multiple display strategies (e.g., dissimulating, expressing as felt, and amplifying). This pattern suggests that the relationship might reflect the diagnostic value attributed to authentic emotional expressions rather than the general prevalence or amplification of emotional displays. Full analyses using the overall expressivity index are reported in the Supplementary Information.

To address concerns that cross-cultural differences in emotion perception might compromise the comparability of automated emotion-resemblance estimates, we compared models allowing sites (i.e., countries; Hong Kong included as a separate site where available) to vary in overall rating intensity (random intercepts) versus models allowing variation in sensitivity to structural cues (random slopes; see Supplementary Information). Model comparison indicated no evidence for cross-site slope variation, supporting the cross-site comparability of Py-Featderived resemblance scores for use as inputs when estimating emotion overgeneralization within each country.

These pilot results illustrate that the proposed operationalization and analytic framework can detect cultural variation in emotion overgeneralization. However, participant sample sizes varied widely across countries in the PSA face perception dataset, with some countries contributing fewer than 10 participants (see Figure S1), making these estimates necessarily preliminary and potentially subject to instability. Meanwhile, the composition of pilot samples likely varied across sites (e.g., student vs. general-population recruitment), introducing heterogeneity beyond sample size that further limits cross-country comparability. The planned study addresses this limitation directly by targeting equal samples of N = 200 per country and recruiting through quota-balanced panels with comparable demographic composition across sites (see Sampling strategy). Moreover, although there was substantial interindividual variation in emotional expressivity norm perception within countries 32 , the available data did not allow us to examine individual-level associations, as the emotional expressivity and face perception datasets were collected at different time points and were not obtained from the same individuals. These considerations underscore the importance of a preregistered, purpose-built investigation. overgeneralization (pilot data; N = 21). Section 1. Pilot analyses

This section provides detailed analytic procedures for the pilot analyses described in the main text. To evaluate the feasibility of the proposed operationalization and analytic framework for testing the main hypothesis H1, we conducted pilot analyses linking cross-cultural emotional expressivity norms to large-scale face-based trustworthiness judgments.

Data sources

Emotional expressivity scores were drawn from a prior cross-cultural study assessing display rule norms across 32 countries and regions (including Hong Kong, a Special Administrative Region of China) using the Display Rule Assessment Inventory (DRAI) 32 . The DRAI measures normative regulation of emotional expression across multiple interaction contexts. The present pilot used the "express it as you feel it" index, defined as the total number of situations in which participants endorsed authentic emotional expression. We selected this index over the broader overall expressivity measure because it more directly captures normative endorsement of genuine emotional display rather than amplification or suppression tendencies. Of the 32 sites in the original dataset, 21 overlapped with the face-rating dataset described below.

Trustworthiness ratings were obtained from the Psychological Science Accelerator (PSA) project 35 , which collected trait judgments across 43 countries and regions using 120 standardized face images from the Chicago Face Database 36 . Participants rated one of 13 traits on a 9-point scale. The present pilot analysis used trustworthiness ratings only. Ratings were provided in the primary language of each country. Among the more than 10,000 participants in the PSA face perception dataset, each participant rated a single trait that was randomly assigned. Of these, 531 participants rated trustworthiness (see Figure S1 for the number of participants by country). Full methodological details of the PSA face perception project are available in Jones et al. 35.

We controlled for national ethnic fractionalization, obtained from Alesina et al. 40 , to account for potential confounding effects of cross-racial familiarity on participants' trait ratings. countries in the Psychological Science Accelerator (PSA) face perception dataset. Country names are shown on the y-axis, and participant counts are indicated at the end of each bar. Bar color and length represent the number of participants contributing trustworthiness ratings in each country, illustrating variation in sample size across cultures.

Operationalization of emotion overgeneralization

For each of the 120 face stimuli, structural resemblance to happiness was estimated using Py-Feat, yielding a probability score reflecting resemblance to happiness-related facial configurations. Emotion overgeneralization was operationalized as the association between happiness resemblance scores and trustworthiness judgments across faces.

Because the expressivity predictor was only available as a national aggregate, we computed overgeneralization using two complementary estimators. First, using raw participant trustworthiness ratings, emotion overgeneralization was calculated separately for each participant by correlating their ratings with the corresponding happiness resemblance scores across faces, with correlations Fisher z-transformed prior to analysis. Second, using an aggregated approach, trustworthiness ratings were first averaged across participants within each country for each face stimulus to reduce participant-level noise. Pearson correlations were then computed between happiness resemblance scores and these aggregated trustworthiness ratings across the 120 faces.

The resulting correlation coefficients were also Fisher z-transformed.

Statistical models

Linear regression of aggregated ratings. Because the emotional expressivity predictor is available only at the national level in this dataset, we first examined the relationship between emotional expressivity and emotion overgeneralization using country-level aggregated ratings (N = 21 countries). Emotion overgeneralization scores were computed by aggregating participant trustworthiness ratings within each country. We estimated ordinary least squares regression models predicting aggregated emotion overgeneralization from national emotional expressivity.

For each expressivity measure, we estimated both a simple model, including emotional expressivity as the sole predictor, and a full model, additionally controlling for ethnic fractionalization. Results from both models are reported in Table S1.

For the "express it as you feel it" index, higher endorsement of expressing emotions as felt was associated with stronger emotion overgeneralization (full model: b = 0.47, 95% CI [0.03, 0.90], SE = 0.21, t(18) = 2.27, p = .036). In contrast, the overall expressivity measure-capturing the extent to which individuals believe they should conceal, freely express, or amplify emotional expressions-was not significantly associated with emotion overgeneralization in either the simple or full models (Table S1).

Mixed-effect analysis of participant ratings. Because the number of participants contributing ratings varied substantially across countries, we additionally analyzed the raw participant ratings to account for differences in sample size across countries (N = 531 participants nested within 21 countries). We estimated linear mixed-effects models predicting emotion overgeneralization from national emotional expressivity with random intercepts for country. As in the aggregated analyses, we estimated both a simple model and a full model controlling for ethnic fractionalization, participant age, and gender. Results from both models are reported in Table S2.

For the "express it as you feel it" index, national emotional expressivity was positively associated with individual-level emotion overgeneralization (full model: b = 0.16, 95% CI [0.01, 0.32], SE = 0.07, t(10.4) = 2.29, p = .044). Covariates were not statistically significant. The random-intercept variance was 0.00085 (ICC = 0.048). The low ICC indicates that the majority of variance in overgeneralization lies within countries, consistent with the expectation that individual differences are substantial. Nevertheless, the significant country-level predictor suggests that national expressivity norms still account for meaningful between-country variation.

These results also underscore the importance of examining within-country variation in expressivity-related individual differences. Consistent with the aggregated analyses, the Overall Expressivity measure was not significantly associated with emotion overgeneralization in either the simple or full models (Table S2).

Together, both estimators converge on the same directional pattern, suggesting that the proposed analytic framework can detect cross-national variation in emotion overgeneralization despite differences in sample size and aggregation level. With substantially larger and more balanced samples per country in the planned study (N = 200 per country), these estimates should be considerably more precise.

Assessment of cross-cultural variability in perceptual mapping

A potential methodological concern is that cultures may differ not only in how much they overgeneralize emotion to trait judgments, but in how they perceive the emotional content of faces in the first place. If the mapping between structural resemblance and perceived emotion varies across countries and regions, then Py-Feat-derived scores would not function as a comparable input across countries, confounding any cross-cultural comparison of overgeneralization.

To ensure that Py-Feat-derived structural resemblance scores are comparably linked to emotion perceptions across countries and regions, we tested whether cross-cultural differences in emotion judgments reflected overall rating intensity (intercepts) or differential sensitivity to structural resemblance (slopes). We compared a random-intercept model with a model including Model comparison indicated no evidence for random slope variation, Δχ²(2) = 0.29, p = .867. These results suggest that cross-cultural differences are primarily reflected in overall rating intensity rather than heterogeneous slopes linking structural emotion resemblance to perceived emotions. Because our primary interest lies in correlations between Py-Feat-derived resemblance scores and trustworthiness judgments, variation in intercepts does not affect our inferences, whereas variation in slopes would. Accordingly, this analysis supports the cross-site comparability of Py-Feat-derived resemblance scores (i.e., measurement consistency) as inputs in our main analyses. Note that this check pertains specifically to the mapping between Py-Featderived resemblance scores and explicit emotion perceptions, and therefore does not address whether emotion overgeneralization (i.e., structural resemblance → trustworthiness) varies across countries, which is the focus of our preregistered hypotheses.

The figure displays the distribution of the 32 sampled countries (dark grey) relative to the global reference distribution (light grey) across three key dimensions: cultural distance from the United States 44 (left panel), GLOBE In-group Collectivism 42 (societal practices; middle panel), and Hofstede's individualism index 43 (right panel). Panels correspond to the number of participants per country (N). Points represent mean power across 1,000 simulations, with vertical lines indicating 95% confidence intervals. Horizontal dashed and dotted lines denote 95% and 90% power thresholds, respectively. Under the planned design (K = 32, N = 200), power reached 95.2% for an expected effect size of b = 0.16. Curves for K = 30 are omitted for visual clarity; this condition was included in the simulations but is not shown in the figure.

Supplementary information

This Supplementary information file includes full details of the pilot analyses (Section 1), a description of the selected countries for data collection (Section 2), and details of the power analyses (Section 3).

Section 2. Selected countries for data collection

Country selection is summarized in Table S3. Countries were sampled to ensure broad geographical coverage and diversity in cultural groupings. Expressivity levels were additionally used to characterize cross-national variation in emotional expressivity. The resulting cultural and psychological diversity of the sample is illustrated in Figure S2, which shows that the selected countries span a wide range of values across key dimensions, including cultural distance from the United States, GLOBE In-group Collectivism, and Hofstede's individualism index.

Section 3. Power analyses

We conducted simulation-based power analyses using the simr package in R 47 , following the planned multilevel models described in the main text. Analyses were implemented using R scripts adapted from prior work 53 , with modifications to match the present design. Full simulation code and output tables are provided to facilitate reproducibility (see the Code Availability section).

All simulations used 1,000 iterations, with statistical significance evaluated at α = .05 and fixed effects tested using the Kenward-Roger approximation. Power was estimated across a range of design parameters, including the number of countries (K = 20, 25, 30, 32), the number of participants per country (N = 150, 200, 250, 300), and plausible effect sizes informed by pilot data and prior literature. For H1, the primary simulation used an expected effect of b = 0.16, based on the pilot study. For H2, we additionally varied the random slope variance for individual-level expressivity (0.0064, 0.0144, 0.0256), corresponding to slope standard deviations of 0.08, 0.12, and 0.16.

Assuming a main effect of b = 0.10, these variance levels correspond to three theoretical distributions: specifically, a variance of 0.0064 (SD = 0.08) models a scenario of baseline heterogeneity, where the effect is positive in approximately 90% of sampled countries. A variance of 0.0144 (SD = 0.12) represents moderate cultural moderation, where the effect is positive in 80% of cultures but reverses in 20%. Finally, a variance of 0.0256 (SD = 0.16) reflects higher cultural moderation, where specific contextual factors (e.g., distinct cultural norms) drive effect reversals in approximately 30% of the countries. Power curves across the full parameter space are presented in Figure S3 (H1) and Figure S4 (H2).

Figure S4.

Power curves for H2 across parameter space. Estimated statistical power for detecting the effect of individual-level expressivity on overgeneralization as a function of random slope variance and number of countries (K). Panels are arranged by effect size (b; rows) and number of participants per country (N; columns). Points represent mean power across 1,000 simulations, with vertical lines indicating 95% confidence intervals. Horizontal dashed and dotted lines denote 95% and 90% power thresholds, respectively. Under the planned design (K = 32, N = 200), power exceeded 95% for detecting a small effect (b = 0.10) under a conservative slope variance assumption (0.0144). Curves for K = 30 are omitted for visual clarity; this condition was included in the simulations but is not shown in the figure.
